The key insight is that we need to build a text classification system. I'll use:
1. **Feature extraction**: Extract meaningful features from the document text
2. **Training**: Collect patterns that distinguish documents by subject
3. **Classification**: Predict subject based on features

Given the constraints and lack of direct training data access, I'll implement a simple approach using common text features:

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Extract text features** from the document:
   - Word frequency analysis
   - Common keywords for each subject
   - Text length patterns
   - Character patterns

2. **Create subject-specific signatures** based on typical vocabulary patterns

3. **Classification algorithm**:
   - Count word frequencies
   - Compare against subject-specific patterns
   - Assign subject based on strongest match

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def extract_text_features(text):
    """Extract features from document text"""
    # Convert to lowercase and remove punctuation
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', ' ', text.lower())
    
    # Split into words
    words = text.split()
    
    # Remove very short words
    words = [word for word in words if len(word) > 2]
    
    # Count word frequencies
    word_count = Counter(words)
    
    # Extract statistical features
    total_words = len(words)
    avg_word_length = sum(len(word) for word in words) / total_words if total_words > 0 else 0
    
    # Extract common n-grams (first few)
    bigrams = []
    for i in range(len(words) - 1):
        bigrams.append(words[i] + " " + words[i+1])
    
    return {
        'word_count': total_words,
        'avg_word_length': avg_word_length,
        'unique_words': len(word_count),
        'top_words': word_count.most_common(10),
        'bigrams': bigrams
    }

def classify_subject(text_features):
    """Classify document based on features using rule-based approach"""
    
    # Create subject-specific keyword patterns
    subject_1_keywords = {
        'technology': ['computer', 'software', 'code', 'program', 'algorithm', 'hardware'],
        'science': ['research', 'experiment', 'data', 'study', 'analyze', 'science'],
        'math': ['equation', 'formula', 'function', 'calculate', 'number', 'mathematics']
    }
    
    subject_2_keywords = {
        'health': ['health', 'medicine', 'treatment', 'patient', 'doctor', 'wellness'],
        'education': ['school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'curriculum', 'learning'],
        'environment': ['nature', 'forest', 'plant', 'animal', 'ecosystem', 'pollution']
    }
    
    subject_3_keywords = {
        'trade': ['trade', 'market', 'business', 'money', 'price', 'purchase'],
        'economics': ['asset', 'investment', 'stock', 'profit', 'finance', 'currency'],
        'industry': ['factory', 'production', 'supply', 'demand', 'product', 'manufacture']
    }
    
    # Count matches for each subject
    score_1 = 0
    score_2 = 0
    score_3 = 0
    
    # Check for keywords
    for word, count in text_features['top_words']:
        for key, keywords in subject_1_keywords.items():
            if word in keywords:
                score_1 += count
        for key, keywords in subject_2_keywords.items():
            if word in keywords:
                score_2 += count
        for key, keywords in subject_3_keywords.items():
            if word in keywords:
                score_3 += count
    
    # Add bonus for document length and word patterns
    total_words = text_features['word_count']
    
    # Rule: Longer documents with technical terms might be subject 1
    if total_words > 50:
        score_1 += 2
    
    # Rule: Documents with health/education terms are likely subject 2
    health_terms = ['health', 'doctor', 'student', 'school', 'medicine']
    education_terms = ['education', 'class', 'teacher', 'learning']
    for word in text_features['top_words']:
        if word[0] in health_terms:
            score_2 += word[1]
        if word[0] in education_terms:
            score_2 += word[1]
    
    # Rule: Documents with trade/business terms are likely subject 3
    trade_terms = ['trade', 'market', 'business', 'money', 'price', 'profit']
    economics_terms = ['economics', 'finance', 'investment', 'stock', 'currency']
    for word in text_features['top_words']:
        if word[0] in trade_terms:
            score_3 += word[1]
        if word[0] in economics_terms:
            score_3 += word[1]
    
    # Final decision based on scores
    if score_1 >= score_2 and score_1 >= score_3:
        return 1
    elif score_2 >= score_1 and score_2 >= score_3:
        return 2
    else:
        return 3

def main():
    # Read input
    try:
        lines = []
        for line in sys.stdin:
            lines.append(line.strip())
        
        # Parse input
        if len(lines) < 3:
            print(1)  # Default fallback
            return
            
        doc_id = int(lines[0])
        doc_name = lines[1]
        text = " ".join(lines[2:]) if len(lines) > 2 else ""
        
        # Extract features
        if text:
            text_features = extract_text_features(text)
            subject = classify_subject(text_features)
        else:
            subject = 1  # Default if no text
        
        print(subject)
        
    except Exception as e:
        # Fallback in case of any error
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

This solution implements a rule-based text classifier that:

1. Extracts features from the document text (word frequencies, statistics)
2. Uses keyword matching to identify which subject the document is most likely related to
3. Applies rules to handle different text patterns
4. Returns the most probable subject (1, 2, or 3)

The classifier works by:
- Looking for keywords specific to each subject
- Assigning points based on keyword matches
- Using document length and word patterns as additional hints
- Making final decisions based on the highest score

This approach should work well for the given problem, especially since the training data is structured by subject and the documents contain text that can be analyzed for subject-specific patterns.

The document identifier is used to make installing the problem more convenient and has no useful information for the participants.

You need to write a program that should indicate the subject for a given document. It is guaranteed that all documents given as input to your program correspond to one of the three subjects of the training set.

Input

The first line contains integer id (0 ≤ id ≤ 106) — the document identifier. The second line contains the name of the document. The third and the subsequent lines contain the text of the document. It is guaranteed that the size of any given document will not exceed 10 kilobytes.

The tests for this problem are divided into 10 groups. Documents of groups 1 and 2 are taken from the training set, but their identifiers will not match the identifiers specified in the training set. Groups from the 3-rd to the 10-th are roughly sorted by the author in ascending order of difficulty (these groups contain documents which aren't present in the training set).

Print an integer from 1 to 3, inclusive — the number of the subject the given document corresponds to.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a document classification problem where each document belongs to one of 3 subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. We're given a training set with documents organized by subject in directories 1, 2, and 3
3. We need to classify a new document by predicting which subject it belongs to
4. The program reads input from stdin and outputs the predicted subject (1, 2, or 3)

## Approach

Since this is a document classification problem, I need to:
1. Extract features from the document text
2. Train a model on the training data to learn patterns that distinguish subjects
3. Apply the model to predict the subject of the new document

Given that we have access to the training data, I'll need to:
- Extract text features from the training documents
- Use these features to build a classifier
- Apply the classifier to the input document

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Feature Extraction**: Extract meaningful features from the text (word frequency, character frequency, document length, etc.)
2. **Training**: Build a classifier (I'll use a simple approach like Naive Bayes or histogram-based classification)
3. **Prediction**: Apply the trained model to new documents

Since we don't have the actual training data accessible in this environment, I'll implement a general solution that would work with the training data structure:

```python
import sys
import os
from collections import defaultdict
import re

def extract_features(text):
    """Extract features from the text document"""
    # Convert to lowercase and remove punctuation
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    
    # Basic features
    word_count = len(text.split())
    char_count = len(text)
    unique_words = len(set(text.split()))
    
    # Frequency of common words (simple heuristic)
    common_words = ["the", "of", "and", "to", "in", "it", "is", "that", "with", "for", "on", "by", "at", "this", "these", "those", "they", "will", "would", "could", "should", "has", "have", "had", "do", "does", "been"]
    common_word_count = sum(1 for word in text.split() if word in common_words)
    
    # Return features as tuple
    return (word_count, char_count, unique_words, common_word_count)

def train_model(training_data):
    """Train a simple classifier on the training data"""
    # Initialize feature counts for each subject
    feature_counts = {1: defaultdict(int), 2: defaultdict(int), 3: defaultdict(int)}
    subject_counts = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    # Extract features and count them by subject
    for subject, documents in training_data.items():
        for doc_text in documents:
            features = extract_features(doc_text)
            feature_counts[subject][features[0]] += 1  # word count
            feature_counts[subject][features[1]] += 1  # char count  
            feature_counts[subject][features[2]] += 1  # unique word count
            feature_counts[subject][features[3]] += 1  # common word count
            
            subject_counts[subject] += 1
    
    return feature_counts, subject_counts

def predict_subject(doc_text, feature_counts, subject_counts):
    """Predict subject for a document based on feature analysis"""
    features = extract_features(doc_text)
    
    # Calculate probability based on feature counts
    probabilities = {}
    
    for subject in [1, 2, 3]:
        total_count = subject_counts[subject]
        if total_count == 0:
            continue
            
        # Calculate likelihood based on feature counts
        likelihood = 1.0
        for i, feature_val in enumerate(features):
            if feature_val == 0:
                continue
                
            # Use frequency in the subject as likelihood
            total_features = sum(feature_counts[subject][f] for f in feature_counts[subject])
            if total_features > 0:
                # Simple probability approximation
                likelihood *= (feature_counts[subject][feature_val] + 1) / (total_features + 10)
                
        probabilities[subject] = likelihood * (1.0 / total_count)
    
    # Return subject with highest probability
    if probabilities:
        return max(probabilities, key=probabilities.get)
    else:
        # Default to subject 1 if no data
        return 1

# Since we don't have actual training data, this is a placeholder
# In a real scenario, we would download and parse the training data

# Read input from stdin
def main():
    # Read document identifier
    try:
        doc_id = int(input().strip())
    except ValueError:
        doc_id = 0
    
    # Read document name
    doc_name = input().strip()
    
    # Read document text
    text = ""
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            if line == "":
                break
            text += line + " "
    except EOFError:
        pass
    
    # Since we don't have the actual training data, 
    # we need to implement a more sophisticated approach
    
    # For this problem, let's use a simple heuristic based on text content
    
    # Common patterns - this is a placeholder for actual training
    if "trade" in text.lower() or "business" in text.lower() or "market" in text.lower():
        print(3)
    elif "science" in text.lower() or "research" in text.lower() or "experiment" in text.lower():
        print(2)
    elif "education" in text.lower() or "school" in text.lower() or "student" in text.lower():
        print(1)
    else:
        # Default to subject 1
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Wait, I realize this approach might not be optimal. Let me provide a more robust solution that would work with the actual training data structure:

```python
import sys
import re

def extract_features(text):
    """Extract basic features from the text"""
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    words = text.split()
    
    return {
        'word_count': len(words),
        'unique_words': len(set(words)),
        'avg_word_length': sum(len(word) for word in words) / len(words) if words else 0,
        'first_word': words[0].lower() if words else "",
        'contains_numbers': any(c.isdigit() for c in text),
        'has_special': any(c.isupper() or c.isdigit() for c in text)
    }

def classify_document():
    # Read input
    try:
        doc_id = int(input().strip())
    except:
        doc_id = 0
        
    doc_name = input().strip()
    
    text = ""
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            if not line:
                break
            text += line + " "
    except:
        pass
    
    # Simple heuristic based on content
    # In a real solution, we would train on the actual training data
    
    # Check for keywords in each subject
    subject_1_keywords = ["education", "school", "student", "teacher", "learning", "class", "study", "academic", "examination"]
    subject_2_keywords = ["science", "research", "experiment", "lab", "nature", "biology", "chemistry", "physics", "engineering"]
    subject_3_keywords = ["trade", "business", "market", "economy", "finance", "investment", "commerce", "deal", "purchase", "sales"]
    
    text_lower = text.lower()
    
    # Count matches
    count_1 = sum(1 for kw in subject_1_keywords if kw in text_lower)
    count_2 = sum(1 for kw in subject_2_keywords if kw in text_lower)
    count_3 = sum(1 for kw in subject_3_keywords if kw in text_lower)
    
    # Assign based on highest count
    if count_1 > count_2 and count_1 > count_3:
        print(1)
    elif count_2 > count_1 and count_2 > count_3:
        print(2)
    elif count_3 > count_1 and count_3 > count_2:
        print(3)
    else:
        # Default to subject 1
        print(1)

classify_document()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a document classification problem where documents belong to one of three subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. We need to predict the subject of a new document based on its text
3. We have a training set with documents already labeled by subject (in directories 1, 2, 3)
4. We need to create a model that can classify new documents

## Approach

Since this is a document classification problem, I'll use a simple approach based on keyword analysis:

1. **Collect training data**: Extract text from all documents in the training directories
2. **Create feature vectors**: Count word frequencies or use keywords that are specific to each subject
3. **Build classification rules**: Use the most characteristic words for each subject to classify new documents

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the problem structure
- Documents have an ID, name, and text content
- Subjects are 1, 2, 3 with text content in separate directories
- We need to build a classifier from the training data

### Step 2: Design the classifier
I'll use a simple rule-based approach:
- Extract common words from documents of each subject
- For a new document, check which subject's keywords appear most frequently

### Step 3: Implementation

```python
import os
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def extract_text_from_directory(directory_path):
    """Extract text from all files in a directory"""
    text_corpus = []
    
    # Read all files in the directory
    for filename in os.listdir(directory_path):
        file_path = os.path.join(directory_path, filename)
        try:
            with open(file_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                lines = file.readlines()
                # Skip empty files
                if lines:
                    # The first line is id, second is name, rest is text
                    text_content = ' '.join(lines[2:]) if len(lines) > 2 else ''
                    if text_content.strip():
                        text_corpus.append(text_content.strip())
        except Exception as e:
            continue
    
    return text_corpus

def build_subject_features():
    """Build feature sets for each subject"""
    # Since we can't actually access the training files from this environment,
    # I'll implement a general approach that would work with real training data.
    
    # In practice, we would:
    # 1. Extract all documents from directories 1, 2, 3
    # 2. Count word frequencies for each subject
    # 3. Identify characteristic words for each subject
    
    # For this problem, I'll implement a simple heuristic-based approach
    # that would work on the actual training data
    
    # Let's define some sample features that might be characteristic of each subject
    # These would be learned from the actual training set
    subject_words = {
        1: ['technology', 'system', 'software', 'engineer', 'network', 'development'],
        2: ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'course', 'academic'],
        3: ['trade', 'market', 'business', 'economy', 'profit', 'investment']
    }
    
    return subject_words

def classify_document(text, subject_words):
    """Classify document based on keyword matching"""
    # Convert text to lowercase and remove punctuation
    text_lower = text.lower()
    text_clean = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text_lower)
    words = text_clean.split()
    
    # Count matches for each subject
    subject_scores = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    for subject, words_list in subject_words.items():
        for word in words_list:
            word_lower = word.lower()
            # Count occurrences of the word in document text
            count = words.count(word_lower)
            subject_scores[subject] += count
    
    # Return subject with highest score
    return max(subject_scores, key=subject_scores.get)

def main():
    # Read input from stdin
    try:
        # Read document identifier
        doc_id = int(input().strip())
        
        # Read document name
        doc_name = input().strip()
        
        # Read document text (all subsequent lines)
        document_text = ""
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                if line:  # Only add non-empty lines
                    document_text += " " + line
        except EOFError:
            pass
        
        # If we don't have any text, set to empty
        if not document_text:
            document_text = ""
        
        # Build subject features (in practice, this would be learned from training data)
        # For this problem, I'll use a simple heuristic approach
        subject_words = {
            1: ['technology', 'system', 'software', 'engineer', 'network', 'development'],
            2: ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'course', 'academic'],
            3: ['trade', 'market', 'business', 'economy', 'profit', 'investment']
        }
        
        # Classify the document
        subject = classify_document(document_text, subject_words)
        
        print(subject)
        
    except Exception as e:
        # Fallback - if any error occurs, return subject 1
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys
import os
from collections import Counter
import re

def preprocess_text(text):
    """Clean and preprocess text to extract meaningful features"""
    # Convert to lowercase
    text = text.lower()
    # Remove punctuation and special characters
    text = re.sub(r'[^a-z\s]', '', text)
    # Remove extra whitespace
    text = ' '.join(text.split())
    return text

def get_document_features(text):
    """Extract features from document text"""
    words = preprocess_text(text).split()
    
    # Remove very short words
    words = [word for word in words if len(word) > 2]
    
    # Create feature vector based on word frequencies and patterns
    features = {
        'total_words': len(words),
        'unique_words': len(set(words)),
        'word_freq': Counter(words),
        'contains_nouns': 0,
        'contains_verbs': 0,
        'contains_adjectives': 0,
        'contains_numbers': 0,
        'has_long_words': 0
    }
    
    # Simple pattern detection for nouns, verbs, adjectives
    for word in words:
        if len(word) > 5:
            features['has_long_words'] = 1
            
        # Check for common suffix patterns (simplified)
        if word.endswith('ing'):
            features['contains_verbs'] += 1
        elif word.endswith('ed'):
            features['contains_verbs'] += 1
        elif word.endswith('s') or word.endswith('es'):
            features['contains_nouns'] += 1
        elif word.endswith('ly'):
            features['contains_adjectives'] += 1
        elif word.isdigit():
            features['contains_numbers'] += 1
    
    return features

def classify_document(text, training_data):
    """
    Classify document based on training data patterns
    training_data should be a dictionary with subjects as keys
    and lists of feature vectors as values
    """
    # For now, we'll use a simple rule-based approach
    # This would be replaced with actual training in a real scenario
    
    # Count occurrences of specific words in each subject
    subject_words = {
        1: set(),
        2: set(),
        3: set()
    }
    
    # Extract words from training data (simulated - in real scenario, we'd load actual data)
    # This is a placeholder - in practice, we would analyze the real training data
    
    # Let's create some simple rules based on content patterns
    text_lower = text.lower()
    
    # Look for keywords associated with each subject
    keywords = {
        1: ['technology', 'computer', 'system', 'software', 'network', 'digital'],
        2: ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'learning'],
        3: ['trade', 'business', 'market', 'purchase', 'sale', 'commerce']
    }
    
    # Score based on keyword matches
    scores = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    for subject, keyword_list in keywords.items():
        for keyword in keyword_list:
            if keyword.lower() in text_lower:
                scores[subject] += 1
    
    # Add additional features
    # Look for specific patterns
    if 'money' in text_lower or 'price' in text_lower or 'cost' in text_lower:
        scores[3] += 2
    
    if 'school' in text_lower or 'student' in text_lower or 'teacher' in text_lower:
        scores[2] += 2
    
    if 'computer' in text_lower or 'code' in text_lower or 'program' in text_lower:
        scores[1] += 2
    
    # Default to subject 1 if no clear indication
    max_score = max(scores.values())
    if max_score == 0:
        return 1
    
    # Return the subject with highest score
    for subject in [1, 2, 3]:
        if scores[subject] == max_score:
            return subject
    
    return 1

def main():
    # Read input
    try:
        # Read document identifier
        doc_id = int(input().strip())
        
        # Read document name
        doc_name = input().strip()
        
        # Read document text
        text_lines = []
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                if line == '':
                    break
                text_lines.append(line)
        except EOFError:
            pass
        
        # Combine all text lines
        full_text = ' '.join(text_lines)
        
        # Classify the document
```

- We need to extract features from the document text
- We'll use a simple approach like counting word frequencies or using more sophisticated text features
- We can use a rule-based approach or a simple machine learning model

## Solution Strategy

1. Count the frequency of words in the document

Actually, let me think about this differently. In a real scenario, we would need the training data. Since we can't access it directly, I'll implement a simple but reasonable approach:

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def pre_process_text(text):
    """Clean and preprocess the text"""
    # Convert to lowercase
    text = text.lower()
    # Remove punctuation and digits
    text = re.sub(r'[^a-z\s]', '', text)
    # Split into words
    words = text.split()
    # Remove empty strings
    words = [word for word in words if word]
    return words

def get_word_frequencies(text):
    """Get frequency count of words in text"""
    words = pre_process_text(text)
    return Counter(words)

def classify_document(text):
    """Classify document based on content"""
    # Get word frequencies from the document
    word_freq = get_word_frequencies(text)
    
    # Simple heuristic: check for common words that might indicate subject
    # This is a simplified pattern matching approach
    # In a real scenario, we would have trained on the actual dataset
    
    # Extract some features
    total_words = sum(word_freq.values())
    if total_words == 0:
        return 2  # default to middle subject
    
    # Simple patterns (these would be learned from training data)
    # For example, if document contains "trade", "market", "stock" -> subject 3
    # if document contains "education", "school", "teacher" -> subject 1
    # if document contains "technology", "computer", "software" -> subject 2
    
    # Look for key phrases
    text_lower = text.lower()
    
    # Check for subject 1 patterns
    subject_1_keywords = ['education', 'school', 'teacher', 'student', 'class', 'academic', 'examination']
    subject_2_keywords = ['technology', 'computer', 'software', 'internet', 'network', 'digital', 'data']
    subject_3_keywords = ['trade', 'market', 'stock', 'business', 'company', 'finance', 'profit', 'sales']
    
    subject_1_score = sum(1 for kw in subject_1_keywords if kw in text_lower)
    subject_2_score = sum(1 for kw in subject_2_keywords if kw in text_lower)
    subject_3_score = sum(1 for kw in subject_3_keywords if kw in text_lower)
    
    # Assign subject based on highest keyword count
    max_score = max(subject_1_score, subject_2_score, subject_3_score)
    
    if max_score == subject_1_score:
        return 1
    elif max_score == subject_2_score:
        return 2
    else:
        return 3

# Read input
id_line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
name_line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
text_lines = []
try:
    while True:
        line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
        if line == '':
            break
        text_lines.append(line)
except:
    pass

# Combine all text lines
document_text = ' '.join(text_lines)

# Classify the document
subject = classify_document(document_text)
print(subject)
```

Note: In a real-world scenario, this would be much more sophisticated (using machine learning on the actual training data). However, since we don't have access to the training set files directly, this provides a reasonable heuristic approach based on common patterns that might exist in documents about education, technology, and trade.

The Smart Beaver from ABBYY came up with another splendid problem for the ABBYY Cup participants! This time the Beaver invites the contest participants to check out a problem on sorting documents by their subjects. Let's describe the problem:

You've got some training set of documents. For each document you know its subject. The subject in this problem is an integer from 1 to 3. Each of these numbers has a physical meaning. For instance, all documents with subject 3 are about trade.

You can download the training set of documents at the following link: http://download4.abbyy.com/a2/X2RZ2ZWXBG5VYWAL61H76ZQM/train.zip. The archive contains three directories with names "1", "2", "3". Directory named "1" contains documents on the 1-st subject, directory "2" contains documents on the 2-nd subject, and directory "3" contains documents on the 3-rd subject. Each document corresponds to exactly one file from some directory.

All documents have the following format: the first line contains the document identifier, the second line contains the name of the document, all subsequent lines contain the text of the document. The document identifier is used to make installing the problem more convenient and has no useful information for the participants.

You need to write a program that should indicate the subject for a given document. It is guaranteed that all documents given as input to your program correspond to one of the three subjects of the training set.

Input

The first line contains integer id (0 ≤ id ≤ 106) — the document identifier. The second line contains the name of the document. The third and the subsequent lines contain the text of the document. It is guaranteed that the size of any given document will not exceed 10 kilobytes.

The tests for this problem are divided into 10 groups. Documents of groups 1 and 2 are taken from the training set, but their identifiers will not match the identifiers specified in the training set. Groups from the 3-rd to the 10-th are roughly sorted by the author in ascending order of difficulty (these groups contain documents which aren't present in the training set).

Output

Print an integer from 1 to 3, inclusive — the number of the subject the given document corresponds to.

Examples

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a document classification problem where each document belongs to one of three subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. We're given a training set with documents organized by subject (directories 1, 2, 3)
3. For a new document, we need to predict which subject it belongs to
4. The documents have a specific format: ID, name, then text content

## Approach

Since this is a machine learning problem, I need to:
1. Extract text features from the training documents
2. Build a classifier (likely using simple rules or pattern matching since it's a competition problem)
3. Apply the classifier to the new document

However, I don't have direct access to the training files (the link is provided but I can't download). Given that this is a competition problem, the solution likely relies on pattern recognition or statistical analysis of text content.

## Key Insights

1. Documents from each subject will have distinct text patterns
2. Since I can't access the actual training data, I need to implement a general approach
3. For competitive programming problems like this, the solution often uses text feature extraction and simple classification

## Proposed Solution

I'll implement a solution that:
1. Extracts key features from the text (word frequencies, common phrases, etc.)
2. Creates simple rules or uses statistical analysis to classify documents

Since I don't have access to the training data, I'll implement a general text classification approach:

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def extract_features(text):
    """Extract features from text for classification"""
    # Convert to lowercase and remove punctuation
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    
    # Extract word frequencies
    words = text.split()
    if not words:
        return Counter()
    
    # Get common words and their frequencies
    word_freq = Counter(words)
    
    # Extract some simple features
    features = {
        'word_count': len(words),
        'unique_words': len(word_freq),
        'common_words': dict(word_freq.most_common(5)),
        'avg_word_length': sum(len(word) for word in words) / len(words) if words else 0
    }
    
    return features

def classify_document(text):
    """Classify document based on features"""
    # Since I don't have access to training data, I'll use a simple heuristic
    # This would be replaced with actual training data analysis
    
    # Look for common keywords that might indicate subject
    keywords = {
        1: ['technology', 'engineer', 'program', 'code', 'software', 'computer', 'network'],
        2: ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'learning', 'instruction'],
        3: ['trade', 'business', 'market', 'finance', 'investment', 'price', 'product']
    }
    
    text_lower = text.lower()
    
    # Count keyword matches
    scores = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    for subject, subject_keywords in keywords.items():
        for keyword in subject_keywords:
            if keyword in text_lower:
                scores[subject] += 1
    
    # Simple rule: if no keywords found, default to subject 1
    if sum(scores.values()) == 0:
        return 1
    
    # Return subject with highest score
    return max(scores, key=scores.get)

# Read input
try:
    # Read document identifier
    id_line = input().strip()
    if not id_line:
        id_line = "0"
    
    # Read document name
    name_line = input().strip()
    
    # Read document text
    text_lines = []
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            if line == "":
                break
            text_lines.append(line)
    except EOFError:
        pass
    
    # Combine all text lines
    document_text = " ".join(text_lines)
    
    # Classify the document
    subject = classify_document(document_text)
    
    # Output the result
    print(subject)
    
except Exception as e:
    # Fallback - if any error occurs, return subject 1
    print(1)
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    # Read inputs
    try:
        doc_id = input().strip()
        doc_name = input().strip()
        
        # Read text content
        text = ""
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                if line == "":
                    break
                text += line + " "
        except EOFError:
            pass
            
        # Clean text

        # Simple text analysis - this would be enhanced with actual training data

        # This is a placeholder - the actual solution would use trained model

        if 'technology' in text or 'program' in text or 'code' in text:
            print(1)
        elif 'education' in text or 'school' in text or 'student' in text:
            print(2)
        elif 'trade' in text or 'business' in text or 'market' in text:
            print(3)
        else:
            # Default to subject 1
            print(1)
            
    except Exception as e:
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
```
